data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1063604349">.mw-parser-output .navbar{display:inline;font-size:88%;font-weight:normal}.mw-parser-output .navbar-collapse{float:left;text-align:left}.mw-parser-output .navbar-boxtext{word-spacing:0}.mw-parser-output .navbar ul{display:inline-block;white-space:nowrap;line-height:inherit}.mw-parser-output .navbar-ct-mini{font-size:114%;margin:0 4em}</style><div class="navbar plainlinks hlist navbar-mini"><ul><li class="nv-view"><a href="/wiki/Template:Gospel_Jesus" title="Template:Gospel Jesus"><abbr title="View this template">v</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-talk"><a href="/wiki/Template_talk:Gospel_Jesus" title="Template talk:Gospel Jesus"><abbr title="Discuss this template">t</abbr></a></li><li class="nv-edit"><a class="external text" href="https://en.wikipedia.org/w/index.php?title=Template:Gospel_Jesus&amp;action=edit"><abbr title="Edit this template">e</abbr></a></li></ul></div></td></tr></tbody></table>
<p>The <b>Massacre of the Innocents</b> is an incident in the <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus" title="Nativity of Jesus">nativity narrative</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a> (<a href="/wiki/Matthew_2:16" title="Matthew 2:16">2:16</a>–<a href="/wiki/Matthew_2:18" title="Matthew 2:18">18</a>) in which <a href="/wiki/Herod_the_Great" title="Herod the Great">Herod the Great</a>, king of <a href="/wiki/Judea#Roman_period" title="Judea">Judea</a>, orders the execution of all male children who are two years old and under in the vicinity of <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a>. Christians venerate them as the first <a href="/wiki/Christian_martyrs" class="mw-redirect" title="Christian martyrs">Christian martyrs</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClarke200322_2-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke200322-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> A majority of Herod biographers, and "probably a majority of [...] biblical scholars," hold the event to be myth or legend.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMaier1998170-171_3-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMaier1998170-171-3">&#91;3&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<meta property="mw:PageProp/toc" />
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Biblical_narrative">Biblical narrative</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=1" title="Edit section: Biblical narrative">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a> tells how the <a href="/wiki/Biblical_Magi" title="Biblical Magi">Magi</a> visit <a href="/wiki/Jerusalem" title="Jerusalem">Jerusalem</a> to seek guidance as to where the king of the Jews has been born; King Herod directs them to Bethlehem and asks them to return to him and report, but they are warned in a dream that Herod wishes to find the child and kill him, and do not do so. Matthew continues:
</p>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r996844942">.mw-parser-output .templatequote{overflow:hidden;margin:1em 0;padding:0 40px}.mw-parser-output .templatequote .templatequotecite{line-height:1.5em;text-align:left;padding-left:1.6em;margin-top:0}</style><blockquote class="templatequote"><p>When Herod realized that he had been outwitted by the Magi, he was furious, and he gave orders to kill all the boys in Bethlehem and its vicinity who were two years old and under, in accordance with the time he had learned from the Magi.</p><div class="templatequotecite">—&#8201;<cite><a rel="nofollow" class="external text" href="https://bible.oremus.org/?passage=Matthew%202:16&amp;version=nrsv">Matthew 2:16</a></cite></div></blockquote>
<p>This is followed by a reference to and quotation from the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Jeremiah" title="Book of Jeremiah">Book of Jeremiah</a> (<a href="/wiki/Jeremiah_31:15" class="mw-redirect" title="Jeremiah 31:15">Jeremiah 31:15</a>) (Jeremiah 31:14 in the Hebrew Bible): "Then what was said through the prophet Jeremiah was fulfilled: A voice is heard in <a href="/wiki/Ramah_in_Benjamin" title="Ramah in Benjamin">Ramah</a>, weeping and great mourning, <a href="/wiki/Rachel" title="Rachel">Rachel</a> weeping for her children and refusing to be comforted, because they are no more." (<a href="/wiki/Matthew_2:17" title="Matthew 2:17">Matthew 2:17</a>-<a href="/wiki/Matthew_2:18" title="Matthew 2:18">18</a>). The relevance of this to the massacre is not immediately apparent, as Jeremiah's next verses go on to speak of hope and restoration.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClarke200323_4-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke200323-4">&#91;4&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="History_and_theology">History and theology</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=2" title="Edit section: History and theology">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<link rel="mw-deduplicated-inline-style" href="mw-data:TemplateStyles:r1033289096"/><div role="note" class="hatnote navigation-not-searchable">Main article: <a href="/wiki/Matthew_2" title="Matthew 2">Matthew 2</a></div>
<p>The story of the massacre is found in no gospel other than Matthew, nor is it mentioned in the surviving works of <a href="/wiki/Nicolaus_of_Damascus" title="Nicolaus of Damascus">Nicolaus of Damascus</a> (who was a personal friend of Herod the Great), nor in <a href="/wiki/Josephus" title="Josephus">Josephus</a>'s <i><a href="/wiki/Antiquities_of_the_Jews" title="Antiquities of the Jews">Antiquities of the Jews</a></i>, despite his recording many of Herod's misdeeds including the murder of three of his own sons.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEClarke200322_2-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEClarke200322-2">&#91;2&#93;</a></sup> The event is alluded to by <a href="/wiki/Macrobius" title="Macrobius">Macrobius</a>, a pagan writing in the early 5th century: "On hearing that the son of Herod, king of the Jews, had been slain when Herod ordered that all boys in Syria under the age of two be killed, [Augustus] said, 'It’s better to be Herod’s pig than his son.'"<sup id="cite_ref-6" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-6">&#91;a&#93;</a></sup> In view of the lack of independent confirmation that the event ever occurred, many scholars consider it folklore inspired by Herod's reputation.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMagness2021126_7-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMagness2021126-7">&#91;6&#93;</a></sup> 
</p><p>The author appears to have modeled the episode on the biblical story of <a href="/wiki/Pharaohs_in_the_Bible" title="Pharaohs in the Bible">Pharaoh</a>'s attempt to kill the Israelite children in the <a href="/wiki/Book_of_Exodus" title="Book of Exodus">Book of Exodus</a>, as told in an expanded version that was current in the 1st century.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTELincoln201344_8-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTELincoln201344-8">&#91;7&#93;</a></sup> In that expanded story, Pharaoh kills the Hebrew children after his scribes warn him of the impending birth of the threat to his crown (i.e., Moses), but Moses' father and mother are warned in a dream that the child's life is in danger and act to save him.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown197811_9-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown197811-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup> Later in life, after Moses has to flee, like Jesus, he returns when those who sought his death are themselves dead.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBrown197811_9-1" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBrown197811-9">&#91;8&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Numbers">Numbers</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=3" title="Edit section: Numbers">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>The Greek liturgy asserts 14,000 Holy Innocents, while an early Syrian list of saints asserts 64,000. <a href="/wiki/Copt" class="mw-redirect" title="Copt">Coptic</a> sources assert 144,000 and that it took place on 29 December.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEMina1907300-_10-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEMina1907300--10">&#91;9&#93;</a></sup> The <i><a href="/wiki/Catholic_Encyclopedia" title="Catholic Encyclopedia">Catholic Encyclopedia</a></i> of 1907–12, recognising that Bethlehem was too small a town to provide such numbers, reduced the victims to between six and twenty children in the town, with a dozen or so more in the surrounding areas.<sup id="cite_ref-11" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-11">&#91;b&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="In_Christian_art">In Christian art</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=4" title="Edit section: In Christian art">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<p>Medieval <a href="/wiki/Liturgical_drama" title="Liturgical drama">liturgical drama</a> recounted Biblical events, including Herod's slaughter of the innocents. <i>The Pageant of the Shearmen and Tailors</i>, performed in Coventry, England, included a haunting song about the episode, now known as the <a href="/wiki/Coventry_Carol" title="Coventry Carol">Coventry Carol</a>. The <i><a href="/wiki/Ordo_Rachelis" title="Ordo Rachelis">Ordo Rachelis</a></i> tradition of four plays includes the Flight into Egypt, Herod's succession by <a href="/wiki/Herod_Archelaus" title="Herod Archelaus">Archelaus</a>, the return from Egypt, as well as the Massacre all centered on Rachel weeping in fulfillment of Jeremiah's prophecy. These events were likewise in one of the medieval <a href="/wiki/N-Town_Plays" title="N-Town Plays">N-Town Plays</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The "<a href="/wiki/Coventry_Carol" title="Coventry Carol">Coventry Carol</a>" is a <a href="/wiki/Christmas_carol" title="Christmas carol">Christmas carol</a> dating from the 16th century. The carol was performed in <a href="/wiki/Coventry" title="Coventry">Coventry</a> in <a href="/wiki/England" title="England">England</a> as part of a <a href="/wiki/Mystery_play" title="Mystery play">mystery play</a> called <i><a href="/wiki/Coventry_Mystery_Plays" title="Coventry Mystery Plays">The Pageant of the Shearmen and Tailors</a></i>. The play depicts the <a href="/wiki/Christmas" title="Christmas">Christmas</a> story from chapter two in the <a href="/wiki/Gospel_of_Matthew" title="Gospel of Matthew">Gospel of Matthew</a>. The carol refers to the Massacre of the Innocents, in which Herod ordered all male infants two years old and under in <a href="/wiki/Bethlehem" title="Bethlehem">Bethlehem</a> to be killed.<sup id="cite_ref-covcar_12-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-covcar-12">&#91;10&#93;</a></sup> The lyrics of this haunting carol represent a mother's lament for her doomed child. It is the only carol that has survived from this play. The author is unknown. The oldest known text was written down by Robert Croo in 1534, and the oldest known printing of the melody dates from 1591.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEStudwell199515_13-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEStudwell199515-13">&#91;11&#93;</a></sup> The carol is traditionally sung <a href="/wiki/A_cappella" title="A cappella">a cappella</a>.
</p><p>The 17th Century <a href="/wiki/Netherlands" title="Netherlands">Dutch</a> <a href="/wiki/Christmas_song" class="mw-redirect" title="Christmas song">Christmas song</a> <i><a href="/wiki/O_Kerstnacht,_schoner_dan_de_dagen" title="O Kerstnacht, schoner dan de dagen">O Kerstnacht, schoner dan de dagen</a></i>, while beginning with a reference to <a href="/wiki/Nativity_of_Jesus" title="Nativity of Jesus">Christmas Night</a>, is about the Massacre of the Innocents. The Dutch <a href="/wiki/Progressive_rock" title="Progressive rock">progressive rock</a> band <a href="/wiki/Focus_(band)" title="Focus (band)">Focus</a> recorded in 1974 the first two verses of the song for their album <i><a href="/wiki/Hamburger_Concerto" title="Hamburger Concerto">Hamburger Concerto</a></i>.
</p><p>The theme of the "Massacre of the Innocents" has provided artists of many nationalities with opportunities to compose complicated depictions of massed bodies in violent action. It was an alternative to the <i>Flight into Egypt</i> in cycles of the <a href="/wiki/Life_of_the_Virgin" title="Life of the Virgin">Life of the Virgin</a>. It decreased in popularity in <a href="/wiki/Gothic_art" title="Gothic art">Gothic art</a>, but revived in the larger works of the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance_art" title="Renaissance art">Renaissance</a>, when artists took inspiration for their "Massacres" from Roman reliefs of the battle of the <a href="/wiki/Lapith" class="mw-redirect" title="Lapith">Lapiths</a> and <a href="/wiki/Centaur" title="Centaur">Centaurs</a> to the extent that they showed the figures heroically nude.<sup id="cite_ref-Getty_14-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Getty-14">&#91;12&#93;</a></sup> The horrific subject matter of the Massacre of the Innocents also provided a comparison of ancient brutalities with the brutalities of the <a href="/wiki/Early_modern_period" title="Early modern period">early modern period</a>, during the period of religious wars that followed the Reformation&#160;&#8211;&#32; <a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Bruegel)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Bruegel)">Bruegel's versions</a> show the soldiers carrying banners with the <a href="/wiki/Habsburg" class="mw-redirect" title="Habsburg">Habsburg</a> double-headed eagle.<sup id="cite_ref-15" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-15">&#91;13&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>The 1590 version by <a href="/wiki/Cornelis_van_Haarlem" title="Cornelis van Haarlem">Cornelis van Haarlem</a> also seems to reflect the violence of the <a href="/wiki/Dutch_Revolt" class="mw-redirect" title="Dutch Revolt">Dutch Revolt</a>. <a href="/wiki/Guido_Reni" title="Guido Reni">Guido Reni</a>'s early (1611) <i><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Guido_Reni)" class="mw-redirect" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Guido Reni)">Massacre of the Innocents</a></i>, in an unusual vertical format, is at Bologna.<sup id="cite_ref-WGA_16-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-WGA-16">&#91;14&#93;</a></sup> The Flemish painter <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Peter Paul Rubens</a> painted the theme more than once. One version, now in Munich, was engraved and reproduced as a painting as far away as colonial Peru.<sup id="cite_ref-Peru_17-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-Peru-17">&#91;15&#93;</a></sup> Another, his grand <i><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Rubens)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Rubens)">Massacre of the Innocents</a></i> is now at the <a href="/wiki/Art_Gallery_of_Ontario" title="Art Gallery of Ontario">Art Gallery of Ontario</a> in <a href="/wiki/Toronto" title="Toronto">Toronto</a>, Ontario. The French painter <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Poussin" title="Nicolas Poussin">Nicolas Poussin</a> painted <i><a href="/wiki/The_Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Poussin)" title="The Massacre of the Innocents (Poussin)">The Massacre of the Innocents</a></i> (1634) at the height of the <a href="/wiki/Thirty_Years%27_War" title="Thirty Years&#39; War">Thirty Years' War</a>.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p><i>The Childermass</i>, after a traditional name for the <a href="#Feast_day">Feast of the Holy Innocents</a>, is the opening novel of <a href="/wiki/Wyndham_Lewis" title="Wyndham Lewis">Wyndham Lewis</a>'s trilogy <i>The Human Age</i>. In the novel <i><a href="/wiki/The_Fall_(Albert_Camus_novel)" class="mw-redirect" title="The Fall (Albert Camus novel)">The Fall</a></i> (<i>La Chute</i>) by <a href="/wiki/Albert_Camus" title="Albert Camus">Albert Camus</a>, the incident is argued by the main character to be the reason why Jesus chose to let himself be crucified—as he escaped the punishment intended for him while many others died, he felt responsible and died in guilt. A similar interpretation is given in <a href="/wiki/Jos%C3%A9_Saramago" title="José Saramago">José Saramago</a>'s controversial <i><a href="/wiki/The_Gospel_According_to_Jesus_Christ" title="The Gospel According to Jesus Christ">The Gospel According to Jesus Christ</a></i>, but there attributed to Joseph, Jesus' stepfather, rather than to Jesus himself. As depicted by Saramago, Joseph knew of Herod's intention to massacre the children of Bethlehem, but failed to warn the townspeople and chose only to save his own child. Guilt-ridden ever after, Joseph finally expiates his sin by letting himself be crucified (an event not narrated in the New Testament).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p><p>The song "Long Way Around The Sea", from the 1999 <a href="/wiki/Christmas_(Low_EP)" title="Christmas (Low EP)">Christmas</a> EP by the indie-rock band <a href="/wiki/Low_(band)" title="Low (band)">Low</a>, tells the story from the perspective of the magi during their journey from Herod to the newborn Jesus, and the warning from the angel not to return.
</p><p>The Massacre is the opening plot used in the 2006 film <i><a href="/wiki/The_Nativity_Story" title="The Nativity Story">The Nativity Story</a></i> (2016).<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> It is also dramatized in season 1 of the television miniseries <i><a href="/wiki/Jesus_of_Nazareth_(miniseries)" class="mw-redirect" title="Jesus of Nazareth (miniseries)">Jesus of Nazareth</a></i> (1977).
</p><p>The Cornish poet <a href="/wiki/Charles_Causley" title="Charles Causley">Charles Causley</a> used the subject for his poem <i>The Innocents' Song</i>, which as a folk song has been performed by <a href="/wiki/Show_of_Hands" title="Show of Hands">Show of Hands</a> with music by <a href="/wiki/Johnny_Coppin" title="Johnny Coppin">Johnny Coppin</a> (on their album <i><a href="/wiki/Witness_(Show_of_Hands_album)" title="Witness (Show of Hands album)">Witness</a></i>); and by Keith Kendrick and Sylvia Needham.<sup class="noprint Inline-Template Template-Fact" style="white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Citation_needed" title="Wikipedia:Citation needed"><span title="This claim needs references to reliable sources. (December 2016)">citation needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Paintings">Paintings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=5" title="Edit section: Paintings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<ul><li><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Bruegel)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Bruegel)"><i>Massacre of the Innocents</i> by the Bruegels</a>. Several versions of <i>The Massacre of the Innocents</i> were painted by <a href="/wiki/Pieter_Bruegel_the_Elder" title="Pieter Bruegel the Elder">Pieter Bruegel the Elder</a> (c. 1565–67) and his son <a href="/wiki/Pieter_Brueghel_the_Younger" title="Pieter Brueghel the Younger">Pieter Brueghel the Younger</a> (into the 17th century).</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Reni)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Reni)">Massacre of the Innocents</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Guido_Reni" title="Guido Reni">Guido Reni</a>, created in 1611 for the <a href="/wiki/Basilica_of_San_Domenico" class="mw-redirect" title="Basilica of San Domenico">Basilica of San Domenico</a> in <a href="/wiki/Bologna" title="Bologna">Bologna</a>, but now in the <a href="/wiki/Pinacoteca_Nazionale_di_Bologna" title="Pinacoteca Nazionale di Bologna">Pinacoteca Nazionale</a> in that city</li>
<li><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Rubens)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Rubens)">Two versions</a> by <a href="/wiki/Peter_Paul_Rubens" title="Peter Paul Rubens">Peter Paul Rubens</a>, painted in 1611–1612 and 1636–1638</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/The_Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Poussin)" title="The Massacre of the Innocents (Poussin)">The Massacre of the Innocents</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Nicolas_Poussin" title="Nicolas Poussin">Nicolas Poussin</a>, painted between 1625 and 1632</li>
<li><i><a href="/wiki/Massacre_of_the_Innocents_(Matteo_di_Giovanni)" title="Massacre of the Innocents (Matteo di Giovanni)">Massacre of the Innocents</a></i> by <a href="/wiki/Matteo_di_Giovanni" title="Matteo di Giovanni">Matteo di Giovanni</a></li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Music">Music</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=6" title="Edit section: Music">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The communion motet for the feast of the holy innocents is the text from Matthew  2:18 (citing Jeremiah 31:15) <i>Vox in Rama</i>. This was set polyphonically by a number of composers of the <a href="/wiki/Renaissance_music" title="Renaissance music">renaissance</a> and <a href="/wiki/Baroque_music" title="Baroque music">baroque</a>, including <a href="/wiki/Jacob_Clemens_non_Papa" title="Jacob Clemens non Papa">Jacob Clemens non Papa</a>, <a href="/wiki/Giaches_de_Wert" title="Giaches de Wert">Giaches de Wert</a>, <a href="/wiki/Heinrich_Sch%C3%BCtz" title="Heinrich Schütz">Heinrich Schütz</a> (in German). 
</p><p><a href="/wiki/Marc-Antoine_Charpentier" title="Marc-Antoine Charpentier">Marc-Antoine Charpentier</a> composed an oratorio <i>Caedes sanctorum innocentium,</i> H.411, for soloists, chorus, 2 violins and continuo (1683–85).
</p>
<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Feast_day">Feast day</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=7" title="Edit section: Feast day">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Dates_by_denomination">Dates by denomination</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=8" title="Edit section: Dates by denomination">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>Today, the date of <b>Holy Innocents' Day</b>, also called the <b>Feast of the Holy Innocents</b> or <b>Childermas</b> or <b>Children's Mass</b>,<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="Which name is used by which denomination? What do the Orthodox use? (December 2022)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> varies.
</p>
<ul><li>27 December for West Syrians (<a href="/wiki/Syriac_Orthodox_Church" title="Syriac Orthodox Church">Syriac Orthodox Church</a>, <a href="/wiki/Syro-Malankara_Catholic_Church" title="Syro-Malankara Catholic Church">Syro-Malankara Catholic Church</a>, and <a href="/wiki/Maronite_Church" title="Maronite Church">Maronite Church</a>)</li>
<li>* <a href="/wiki/December_28" title="December 28">28 December</a> is the <a href="/wiki/Calendar_of_saints_(Church_of_England)" title="Calendar of saints (Church of England)">date</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Church_of_England" title="Church of England">Church of England</a> (<a href="/wiki/Festival_(Anglicanism)" title="Festival (Anglicanism)">Festival</a>),<sup id="cite_ref-18" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-18">&#91;16&#93;</a></sup> the <a href="/wiki/Lutheran_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Lutheran Church">Lutheran Church</a> and the <a href="/wiki/Roman_Rite" title="Roman Rite">Roman Rite</a> of the <a href="/wiki/Catholic_Church" title="Catholic Church">Catholic Church</a>. In these <a href="/wiki/Western_Christianity" title="Western Christianity">Western Christian</a> denominations, Childermas is the <a href="/wiki/Twelve_Days_of_Christmas" title="Twelve Days of Christmas">fourth day</a> of <a href="/wiki/Christmastide" title="Christmastide">Christmastide</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-19" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-19">&#91;17&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>29 December for the <a href="/wiki/Eastern_Orthodox_Church" title="Eastern Orthodox Church">Eastern Orthodox Church</a>.<sup id="cite_ref-20" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-20">&#91;18&#93;</a></sup></li>
<li>10 January for East Syrians (<a href="/wiki/Chaldean_Catholic_Church" title="Chaldean Catholic Church">Chaldeans</a> and <a href="/wiki/Syro-Malabar_Catholic_Church" class="mw-redirect" title="Syro-Malabar Catholic Church">Syro-Malabar Catholic Church</a>).</li></ul>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Beginnings">Beginnings</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=9" title="Edit section: Beginnings">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>The commemoration of the massacre of the Holy Innocents, traditionally regarded as the first Christian <a href="/wiki/Martyr" title="Martyr">martyrs</a>, if unknowingly so,<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTESmithCheetham1875839-_21-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTESmithCheetham1875839--21">&#91;19&#93;</a></sup><sup id="cite_ref-22" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-22">&#91;c&#93;</a></sup> first appears as a feast of the <a href="/wiki/Western_church" class="mw-redirect" title="Western church">Western church</a> in the <a href="/wiki/Leonine_Sacramentary" class="mw-redirect" title="Leonine Sacramentary">Leonine Sacramentary</a>, dating from about 485. The earliest commemorations<sup class="noprint Inline-Template" style="margin-left:0.1em; white-space:nowrap;">&#91;<i><a href="/wiki/Wikipedia:Please_clarify" title="Wikipedia:Please clarify"><span title="Whose? Entire early Christianity, early Western, early Eastern Orthodox use? Once clarified: pls. adapt the section heading accordingly. (December 2022)">clarification needed</span></a></i>&#93;</sup> were connected with the <a href="/wiki/Epiphany_(holiday)" title="Epiphany (holiday)">Feast of the Epiphany</a>, 6 January: <a href="/wiki/Prudentius" title="Prudentius">Prudentius</a> mentions the Innocents in his hymn on the Epiphany. <a href="/wiki/Pope_Leo_the_Great" class="mw-redirect" title="Pope Leo the Great">Leo</a> in his homilies on the Epiphany speaks of the Innocents. <a href="/wiki/Fulgentius_of_Ruspe" title="Fulgentius of Ruspe">Fulgentius of Ruspe</a> (6th century) gives a homily <i>De Epiphania, deque Innocentum nece et muneribus magorum</i> ("On Epiphany, and on the murder of the Innocents and the gifts of the Magi").<sup id="cite_ref-23" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-23">&#91;d&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Catholic_medieval_traditions">Catholic medieval traditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=10" title="Edit section: Catholic medieval traditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>From the time of <a href="/wiki/Charlemagne" title="Charlemagne">Charlemagne</a>, <a href="/wiki/Sicarius_of_Brant%C3%B4me" title="Sicarius of Brantôme">Sicarius of Bethlehem</a> was venerated at <a href="/wiki/Brant%C3%B4me,_Dordogne" title="Brantôme, Dordogne">Brantôme, Dordogne</a> as one of the purported victims of the Massacre.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEWasyliw200846_24-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEWasyliw200846-24">&#91;20&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In the <a href="/wiki/Middle_Ages" title="Middle Ages">Middle Ages</a>, especially north of the <a href="/wiki/Alps" title="Alps">Alps</a>, the day was a festival of inversion involving <a href="/wiki/Role_reversal" title="Role reversal">role reversal</a> between children and adults such as teachers and priests, with <a href="/wiki/Boy_bishop" title="Boy bishop">boy bishops</a> presiding over some church services.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEHolweck1910_25-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEHolweck1910-25">&#91;21&#93;</a></sup> Bonnie Blackburn and Leofranc Holford-Strevens suggest that this was a Christianized version of the Roman annual feast of the <a href="/wiki/Saturnalia" title="Saturnalia">Saturnalia</a> (when even slaves played "masters" for a day). In some regions, such as medieval England and France, it was said to be an unlucky day, when no new project should be started.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEBlackburnHolford-Strevens1999537–538_26-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEBlackburnHolford-Strevens1999537–538-26">&#91;22&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>There was a medieval custom of refraining where possible from work on the day of the week on which the feast of "Innocents Day" had fallen for the whole of the following year until the next Innocents Day. <a href="/wiki/Philippe_de_Commynes" class="mw-redirect" title="Philippe de Commynes">Philippe de Commynes</a>, the minister of King <a href="/wiki/Louis_XI_of_France" class="mw-redirect" title="Louis XI of France">Louis XI of France</a> tells in his memoirs how the king observed this custom, and describes the trepidation he felt when he had to inform the king of an emergency on the day.<sup id="cite_ref-FOOTNOTEde_Commynes1972253–254_27-0" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-FOOTNOTEde_Commynes1972253–254-27">&#91;23&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span class="mw-headline" id="Catholic_contemporary_traditions">Catholic contemporary traditions</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=11" title="Edit section: Catholic contemporary traditions">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In <a href="/wiki/Spain" title="Spain">Spain</a>, <a href="/wiki/Hispanic_America" title="Hispanic America">Hispanic America</a>, and the <a href="/wiki/Philippines" title="Philippines">Philippines</a>,<sup id="cite_ref-28" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-28">&#91;24&#93;</a></sup> December 28 is still a day for <a href="/wiki/Practical_joke" title="Practical joke">pranks</a>, <a href="/wiki/April_Fools%27_Day#Comparable_prank_days" title="April Fools&#39; Day">equivalent to April Fool's Day</a> in many countries. Pranks (<i>bromas</i>) are also known as <i>inocentadas</i> and their victims are called <i>inocentes</i>; alternatively, the pranksters are the "<i>inocentes</i>" and the victims should not be angry at them, since they could not have committed any <i><a href="/wiki/Sin" title="Sin">sin</a></i>. One of the more famous of these traditions is the annual "<a href="/wiki/Els_Enfarinats" title="Els Enfarinats">Els Enfarinats</a>" festival of <a href="/wiki/Ibi,_Spain" title="Ibi, Spain">Ibi</a> in <a href="/wiki/Alicante" title="Alicante">Alacant</a>, where the <i>inocentadas</i> dress up in full military dress and incite a flour fight.<sup id="cite_ref-29" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-29">&#91;25&#93;</a></sup>
</p><p>In <a href="/wiki/Trinidad_and_Tobago" title="Trinidad and Tobago">Trinidad and Tobago</a>, Roman Catholic children have their toys blessed at a Mass.<sup id="cite_ref-30" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-30">&#91;26&#93;</a></sup>
</p>
<h3><span id="Roman_Rite_before_.26_after_1955"></span><span class="mw-headline" id="Roman_Rite_before_&amp;_after_1955">Roman Rite before &amp; after 1955</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=12" title="Edit section: Roman Rite before &amp; after 1955">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h3>
<p>In the Roman Rite prior to 1955, a unique feature of this feast was the use of liturgical elements ordinarily ascribed to penitential days—including violet vestments, the omission of the <a href="/wiki/Gloria_in_excelsis_Deo" title="Gloria in excelsis Deo">Gloria</a>, and the substitution of a Tract in place of the Alleluia—unless the feast fell on Sunday, in which case the rubrics required the feast to be celebrated as on its octave day, with red vestments, Gloria, and Alleluia. The octave of this feast was suppressed by <a href="/wiki/Pope_Pius_XII" title="Pope Pius XII">Pope Pius XII</a> in 1955, with the feast now celebrated using the features formerly ascribed to its octave day, a practice reinforced by the 1960 <a href="/wiki/Code_of_Rubrics" title="Code of Rubrics">Code of Rubrics</a>.
</p>

<h2><span class="mw-headline" id="Notes">Notes</span><span class="mw-editsection"><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">[</span><a href="/w/index.php?title=Massacre_of_the_Innocents&amp;action=edit&amp;section=15" title="Edit section: Notes">edit</a><span class="mw-editsection-bracket">]</span></span></h2>
<style data-mw-deduplicate="TemplateStyles:r1011085734">.mw-parser-output .reflist{font-size:90%;margin-bottom:0.5em;list-style-type:decimal}.mw-parser-output .reflist .references{font-size:100%;margin-bottom:0;list-style-type:inherit}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-2{column-width:30em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns-3{column-width:25em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns{margin-top:0.3em}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns ol{margin-top:0}.mw-parser-output .reflist-columns li{page-break-inside:avoid;break-inside:avoid-column}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-alpha{list-style-type:upper-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-upper-roman{list-style-type:upper-roman}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-alpha{list-style-type:lower-alpha}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-greek{list-style-type:lower-greek}.mw-parser-output .reflist-lower-roman{list-style-type:lower-roman}</style><div class="reflist reflist-lower-alpha">
<div class="mw-references-wrap"><ol class="references">
<li id="cite_note-6"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-6">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Translation from <i><a href="/wiki/Saturnalia_(Macrobius)" title="Saturnalia (Macrobius)">Saturnalia</a></i> II.IV.11: "Cum audisset inter pueros quos in Syria Herodes rex Iudaeorum intra bimatum iussit interfici filium quoque eius occisum, ait [Augustus]: Melius est Herodis porcum esse quam filium." Retrieved from the University of Chicago<sup id="cite_ref-5" class="reference"><a href="#cite_note-5">&#91;5&#93;</a></sup></span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-11"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-11">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="#CITEREFHolweck1910">Holweck 1910</a> states "The Greek Liturgy asserts that Herod killed 14,000 boys (<i>ton hagion id chiliadon Nepion</i>), the Syrians speak of 64,000, many medieval authors of 144,000, according to Apocalypse 14:3. Writers who accept the historicity of the episode reduce the number considerably, since Bethlehem was a rather small town. Joseph Knabenbauer brings it down to fifteen or twenty (<i>Evang. S. Matt.</i>, I, 104), August Bisping to ten or twelve (<i>Evang. S. Matt.</i>), <a href="/wiki/Lorenz_Kellner" title="Lorenz Kellner">Lorenz Kellner</a> to about six (<i>Christus und seine Apostel</i>, Freiburg, 1908)".</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-22"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-22">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text"><a href="/wiki/Irenaeus" title="Irenaeus">Irenaeus</a> (<i>Adv. Haer.</i> iii.16.4) and <a href="/wiki/Cyprian" title="Cyprian">Cyprian</a> (<i>Epistle</i> 56)</span>
</li>
<li id="cite_note-23"><span class="mw-cite-backlink"><b><a href="#cite_ref-23">^</a></b></span> <span class="reference-text">Prudentius, Leo, and Fulgentius are noted in <a href="#CITEREFSmithCheetham1875">Smith &amp; Cheetham 1875</a>, pp.&#160;839ff. </span>
</li>
</ol></div></div>
